Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I saved `invert.py` in the current folder and ran it. It reconstructs the full 512-sample signal with an SNR of about 54.3 dB against `xtrue`.

**What I chose**
- **Model:** your signal is a sum of `cos(π f (2t+1)/(2n))` terms, which are exactly the DCT-II basis functions. So it has only 12 nonzero DCT coefficients out of 512. I took the DCT coefficients `c` as the unknowns and wrote `x = D.H @ c`, where `D` is `pylops.signalprocessing.DCT(n, type=2)`.
- **Operator:** `A = R @ D.H`, which maps the sparse coefficients to the 128 recorded samples. This is the usual compressed-sensing setup.
- **Solver:** `pylops.optimization.sparsity.fista` with an L1 penalty (`eps=1e-2`, up to 1000 iterations). It stopped after 812 iterations. I chose L1 because the problem is underdetermined (128 equations, 512 unknowns) and the signal is known to be sparse in this basis. A plain least-squares solve would not pick the sparse solution.
- **Output:** `xinv = D.H @ c`, the full time-domain signal. The SNR is computed on this signal against `xtrue`, not on the coefficients.

**How well it worked**
- The last line of the output is `SNR_DB=54.33523343861364`.
- The solution has exactly 12 significant coefficients, which matches the 12 true frequencies.
- I did not tune `eps` or compare it with other settings. The result is for this one seed and noise level (0.005).

import numpy as np
import pylops

rng = np.random.default_rng(7)
n = 512
t = np.arange(n)
freqs = rng.choice(np.arange(5, 200), 12, replace=False)
amps = rng.uniform(0.5, 2, 12) * rng.choice([-1, 1], 12)
xtrue = sum(a * np.cos(np.pi * f * (2 * t + 1) / (2 * n)) for a, f in zip(amps, freqs))

iava = np.sort(rng.choice(n, 128, replace=False))
R = pylops.Restriction(n, iava)
y = R @ xtrue + 0.005 * rng.standard_normal(128)

# Signal is sparse in the DCT-II domain: x = D^H c, y = R D^H c
D = pylops.signalprocessing.DCT(n, type=2)
A = R @ D.H

# Sparse recovery with FISTA (L1)
eps = 1e-2
c, niter, _ = pylops.optimization.sparsity.fista(A, y, eps=eps, niter=1000, show=False)
xinv = D.H @ c
xinv = np.real(xinv)

print("nonzeros (>1e-3*max):", int(np.sum(np.abs(c) > 1e-3 * np.abs(c).max())), "of true 12")
print(f"iterations: {niter}")
print(f"SNR_DB={pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

**What I chose**
- **Model:** your signal is a sum of `cos(π f (2t+1)/(2n))` terms, which are exactly the DCT-II basis functions. So it has only 12 nonzero DCT coefficients out of 512. I took the DCT coefficients `c` as the unknowns and wrote `x = D.H @ c`, where `D` is `pylops.signalprocessing.DCT(n, type=2)`.
- **Operator:** `A = R @ D.H`, which maps the sparse coefficients to the 128 recorded samples. This is the usual compressed-sensing setup.
- **Solver:** `pylops.optimization.sparsity.fista` with an L1 penalty (`eps=1e-2`, up to 1000 iterations). It stopped after 812 iterations. I chose L1 because the problem is underdetermined (128 equations, 512 unknowns) and the signal is known to be sparse in this basis. A plain least-squares solve would not pick the sparse solution.
- **Output:** `xinv = D.H @ c`, the full time-domain signal. The SNR is computed on this signal against `xtrue`, not on the coefficients.

I wrote `invert.py` and ran it. It reconstructs the full 512-sample signal with an SNR of about 65 dB against `xtrue`.

**What I chose**
- **Sparsity basis:** your signal is a sum of 12 cosines of the form `cos(π f (2t+1)/(2n))`. Those are DCT-II basis functions, so the signal is sparse in the DCT domain. I used `pylops.signalprocessing.DCT` (type 2) as the sparsifying transform. Its adjoint, `D.H`, maps DCT coefficients back to the time signal.
- **Operator:** `A = R * D.H` takes DCT coefficients to the 128 recorded samples.
- **Sparse solve:** I used FISTA (`pylops.optimization.sparsity.fista`) to solve the L1-regularized problem `min ‖y − A c‖² + ε‖c‖₁` with `eps=1e-2`. L1 regularization is the right fit because the problem is underdetermined (128 samples for 512 unknowns) and the true coefficients are sparse.
- **Debiasing:** L1 shrinks the coefficient amplitudes, so I kept the coefficients above 5% of the largest one. I then re-fit those by plain least squares and computed `xinv = D.H @ cdeb`.

**How it worked**
- The support detection found exactly 12 coefficients, matching the 12 true frequencies.
- The script's last line is `SNR_DB=65.04684307203243`. This is `pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, xinv)` on the full 512-sample time-domain signal, not on the coefficients.

I made two choices you may want to change. The 5% threshold for the support and `eps=1e-2` were set for this synthetic setup. With real data and a different noise level, you would probably need to retune them.

The installed pylops (2.8.0) has no `norm` argument on `DCT`. The script uses its default.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
import numpy as np
import pylops

rng = np.random.default_rng(7)
n = 512
t = np.arange(n)
freqs = rng.choice(np.arange(5, 200), 12, replace=False)
amps = rng.uniform(0.5, 2, 12) * rng.choice([-1, 1], 12)
xtrue = sum(a * np.cos(np.pi * f * (2 * t + 1) / (2 * n)) for a, f in zip(amps, freqs))

# only 128 randomly located samples were recorded
iava = np.sort(rng.choice(n, 128, replace=False))
R = pylops.Restriction(n, iava)
y = R @ xtrue + 0.005 * rng.standard_normal(128)

# Signal is sparse in the DCT-II domain: x = D^H c, with c sparse.
# Solve min ||y - R D^H c||_2^2 + eps ||c||_1 with FISTA, then map back.
D = pylops.signalprocessing.DCT((n,), type=2)
A = R * D.H
eps = 1e-2
cinv = pylops.optimization.sparsity.fista(A, y, niter=2000, eps=eps, tol=1e-10, show=False)[0]
# Debias: least squares restricted to the detected support
supp = np.abs(cinv) > 0.05 * np.abs(cinv).max()
cdeb = np.zeros(n)
Ssub = pylops.Restriction(n, np.where(supp)[0])
cdeb[supp] = np.linalg.lstsq((A * Ssub.H).todense(), y, rcond=None)[0]
xinv = D.H @ cdeb

print(f"support size: {supp.sum()} (true 12)")
print(f"SNR_DB={pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import fista

rng = np.random.default_rng(7)
n = 512
t = np.arange(n)
freqs = rng.choice(np.arange(5, 200), 12, replace=False)
amps = rng.uniform(0.5, 2, 12) * rng.choice([-1, 1], 12)
xtrue = sum(a * np.cos(np.pi * f * (2 * t + 1) / (2 * n)) for a, f in zip(amps, freqs))

iava = np.sort(rng.choice(n, 128, replace=False))
R = pylops.Restriction(n, iava)
y = R @ xtrue + 0.005 * rng.standard_normal(128)

# Signal is a sum of DCT-II cosines -> sparse in the DCT domain.
D = pylops.signalprocessing.DCT(n, type=2)   # orthonormal; D.H synthesizes signal
A = R * D.H

c, niter, _ = fista(A, y, niter=500, eps=1e-2, eigsdict=dict(niter=50, tol=1e-4), show=False)
xinv = D.H @ c

print(f"nonzero coeffs: {np.sum(np.abs(c) > 0.05 * np.abs(c).max())} (true 12)")
print(f"SNR_DB={pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

Judge votes✓ ✓ ✓
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
**Cause: your `adjoint` is not the adjoint of `forward`.**

Your `forward` is a causal, truncated convolution, `y[i] = Σ_j h[i-j] x[j]`. Its adjoint is a correlation, `x[j] = Σ_i h[i-j] y[i]`. That means convolving with the time-reversed filter and keeping the part that lines up with the model. Your `adjoint` repeats the same causal convolution, which is a different operator. It would only be correct if `h` were symmetric and the filter wasn't truncated.

**Evidence (all run, PyLops 2.8.0, NumPy 2.0.2):**

| | dot-test | cgls result |
|---|---|---|
| your adjoint | fails (`50.19` vs `24.56` in the first run) | `istop=2`, 100 iterations, cost goes from 3.0 up to 20.4, relative error 3.19 |
| fixed adjoint | passes | `istop=1`, stops after 34 iterations, cost goes from 3.0 to 0.005, relative error 0.014 |

With a wrong adjoint, cgls is no longer descending the least-squares cost. The cost can't fall, and the final model has a larger error than the zero model (relative error 3.19 against 1.0).

**Fix:**

```python
def adjoint(y):
    return np.convolve(y, h[::-1], mode="full")[len(h) - 1 : len(h) - 1 + nt]
```

I tested this exact function. Your original slice `[:nt]` on the reversed filter would also be wrong, because the offset of `len(h)-1` is needed.

Keep `pylops.utils.dottest(Op, nt, nt)` as a permanent check next to the operator, and don't loosen its tolerance. If you'd rather not write the adjoint by hand, `pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nt, h, offset=0)` already provides a verified one. I didn't test that route.

I only checked the dot-test on the full operator, which is fine here because there are no blocks. The other checks (shapes, dtypes, scaling) weren't needed once the fix brought the error down to 1.4%.

Run 2 100% 4 turns · $0.09 · 15:15:50 UTC
✓ pass answer

judge votes: PASS PASS PASS

Judge votes✓ ✓ ✓
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
**Cause: your `adjoint` is the forward operator again, not its adjoint.**

`forward` is multiplication by the lower-triangular Toeplitz matrix `H`. Its adjoint is `Hᴴ`, which is upper-triangular. That means correlation with `h`, not convolution. Your `adjoint` runs the same causal convolution as `forward`. `H` isn't symmetric, so this computes `H y` where `Hᴴ y` is needed. The "adjoint" also delays the signal when it should advance it.

**What I ran** (PyLops 2.8.0, NumPy 2.0.2, SciPy 1.13.1)

| | dot-test | cgls result | relative error |
|---|---|---|---|
| your code | **False** | stopped at `istop=2` after 100 iterations | **3.19** (worse than returning zeros) |
| fixed adjoint | True | `istop=1` after 34 iterations | **0.0143** |

cgls uses `Opᴴ` to build its search directions. With a wrong adjoint it isn't minimizing the least-squares cost, so the cost stalls and the model is wrong.

**Fix**

```python
def adjoint(y):
    # correlation with h = adjoint of the causal convolution truncated to nt
    return np.correlate(np.pad(y, (0, len(h) - 1)), h, mode="valid")[:nt]
```

Equivalent: `np.convolve(y[::-1], h)[:nt][::-1]` is **not** quite right, because the truncation changes. Use the correlate version above, which I tested.

The padding matters. `H[i, j] = h[i-j]` for `0 ≤ i-j < len(h)`, so `(Hᴴy)[j] = Σ_k h[k] y[j+k]`. Samples past the end of `y` count as zero.

Alternatively, skip the hand-written adjoint: `pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nt, h, offset=0)` is built-in and already dot-tested. Its output is the "same" size with `offset` selecting the filter's zero-lag sample, so with `offset=0` it should match your causal filter. I haven't run that variant.

**Checks not run:** I didn't go through the shape, dtype, NaN, conditioning or regularization checks. After the fix the result is accurate (1.4% error with noise at 1e-3), so they weren't needed. The `damp=1e-3` and `niter=100` settings are fine, since the solver stops on tolerance at 34 iterations.

Judge votes✓ ✓ ✓
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
**Cause: your `adjoint` is not the adjoint of `forward`.** It repeats the forward convolution. The adjoint of a causal convolution is a correlation, which is an anti-causal filter. CGLS needs a true adjoint, so it was solving the wrong problem.

**Check 1, dot-test (run):**
- Your version fails: `pylops.utils.dottest(Op, 300, 300)` raises `AssertionError`.
- The corrected version passes.
- I also built the dense matrix `F` from `forward` and confirmed that the corrected adjoint equals `F.T`.

**Effect on the inversion (run, `cgls`, `niter=100`, `damp=1e-3`, PyLops 2.8.0):**

| adjoint | iterations (`istop`) | final cost | relative error |
|---|---|---|---|
| yours | 100 (`istop=2`) | 20.4 (up from 3.0 at the start) | 3.19 |
| corrected | 34 (`istop=1`, tolerance reached) | 0.0051 | 0.0143 |

**Fix:** use correlation for the adjoint. It is the same as flipping `h` and padding the end of `y` with zeros:

```python
def adjoint(y):
    ypad = np.concatenate([y, np.zeros(len(h) - 1)])
    return np.correlate(ypad, h, mode="valid")[:nt]
    # equivalently: np.convolve(ypad, h[::-1], mode="valid")
```

Check this with the dot-test before you invert:

```python
pylops.utils.dottest(Op, nt, nt, verb=True)
```

You can also skip the hand-written adjoint. `pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nt, h, offset=0)` does the same causal filtering and has a correct, tested adjoint.

**Other checks:** I did not test shapes, dtypes, NaNs, scaling or regularisation separately. With the corrected adjoint the error is 1.4%, so I saw no need to.

Judge votes✓ ✓ ✓
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
**Your `adjoint` is the same function as `forward`, and for a causal filter that isn't the adjoint.**

The forward operator is `y[n] = Σ_k h[k] x[n-k]`, a lower-triangular Toeplitz matrix `A`. Its adjoint is `Aᵀ`, which is upper-triangular. It is a correlation with `h`, where `x[m] = Σ_k h[k] y[m+k]`. It looks forward in time, whereas your `adjoint` still looks backward. So `adjoint` applies `A` again instead of `Aᵀ`.

That explains both symptoms:

- **`dottest` fails** because `⟨Ax, y⟩ ≠ ⟨x, Ay⟩` when `A ≠ Aᵀ`.
- **`cgls` stalls** because it assumes the `adjoint` you gave it is `Aᴴ`. With the wrong one, its search directions aren't gradients of the least-squares cost, so the cost decreases for a few iterations and then stops.

**Fix:** correlate instead of convolve, which means convolving with the flipped filter and picking the right window.

```python
L = len(h)

def adjoint(y):
    # A^T y : x[m] = sum_k h[k] y[m+k]
    return np.convolve(y, h[::-1], mode="full")[L - 1 : L - 1 + nt]
```

Then `pylops.utils.dottest(Op, nt, nt)` should pass. Alternatively, `np.correlate(y, h, mode="full")[L-1:]` gives the same result, padded or truncated to length `nt`.

If you don't need a custom function, `pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nt, h=h, offset=0)` is a built-in operator with a correct adjoint. With `offset=0` the filter acts causally, as in your code.

I haven't run the corrected code. Run `dottest` first as a check. After the fix, `cgls` should converge much better. Because the filter is a smooth decaying exponential, the problem is ill-conditioned, so `damp` or more iterations may still matter for noise.

Judge votes✓ ✓ ✓
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The adjoint is wrong. `cgls` needs `adjoint` to be the true transpose of `forward`, and yours isn't. It just repeats the forward convolution.

**Why it fails**

Your forward operator is a lower-triangular Toeplitz matrix, `y[n] = Σ_k h[k] x[n-k]`. The matrix isn't symmetric, so its transpose is a correlation, not a convolution:

```
x[m] = Σ_k h[k] y[m+k]
```

Using `np.convolve(y, h)[:nt]` applies `H` a second time instead of `Hᵀ`. That makes the dot test fail, because `<Hx, y> ≠ <x, Hy>`. CGLS also assumes it is working with `Hᵀ`, so its search directions are wrong and the cost stalls.

**Fix**

Convolve with the flipped filter and take the matching slice. This is the same thing as `np.correlate`:

```python
L = len(h)

def adjoint(y):
    return np.convolve(y, h[::-1], mode="full")[L - 1 : L - 1 + nt]
```

Check the indexing: with `j = m + L - 1`, `np.convolve(y, h[::-1])[j] = Σ_i y[i] h[i-m]`, which is the formula above. Terms with `i-m` outside `0..L-1` drop out.

Then the dot test should pass:

```python
pylops.utils.dottest(Op, nt, nt, verb=True)  # True
```

**Alternative**

You could skip the hand-written adjoint. `pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nt, h, offset=0)` is a built-in operator for this, with a correct adjoint. With a causal filter, `offset=0` puts the filter's first sample at lag 0. Check the output against your `forward` to confirm the offset convention.

I haven't run this. The derivation is short enough that I'm confident in it, but run the dot test to confirm. Once the adjoint is right, `cgls` should converge. The recovery will still be smooth, because the exponential filter attenuates high frequencies, so spikes won't come back perfectly sharp. If you want sparse spikes, consider `pylops.optimization.sparsity.fista` or `ista` with an L1 penalty.

Judge votes✓ ✓ ✓
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The adjoint is wrong. A causal convolution isn't self-adjoint, so `adjoint` can't be the same convolution as `forward`.

Your `forward` is a lower-triangular Toeplitz matrix, `H[n, m] = h[n-m]` for `n >= m`. Its adjoint is `Hᵀ`, which is upper triangular: `(Hᵀy)[m] = Σₖ h[k]·y[m+k]`. That is a correlation with `h` (an anti-causal filter), not a convolution. Your `adjoint` applies `H` again, so `dottest` fails. CGLS also assumes the `rmatvec` it gets is the true adjoint. With a wrong one, the search directions don't correspond to the gradient of the cost, so it stalls.

Fix:

```python
nh = len(h)

def adjoint(y):
    return np.correlate(y, h, mode="full")[nh - 1 : nh - 1 + nt]
```

`np.correlate(y, h, "full")[nh-1+m]` equals `Σₖ y[m+k]·h[k]`, which matches `Hᵀy`. An equivalent form is `np.convolve(y, h[::-1])[nh-1 : nh-1+nt]`.

Keep `forward` as is. Then `pylops.utils.dottest(Op, nt, nt)` should pass, and `cgls` should converge.

Two other things to check:
- **Easier route:** `pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nt, h, offset=0)` (or `pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D` with `method="direct"`) is a built-in with a correct adjoint, so you don't need to write it by hand.
- **Conditioning:** `h` is a smooth decaying filter, so `H` is ill-conditioned. With noise and a sparse `xtrue`, a plain CGLS solution may still be rough at 100 iterations. If it is, use a larger `damp` or a sparsity-promoting solver such as `pylops.optimization.sparsity.fista`.

I haven't run this. Once you change the adjoint, rerun `dottest` to confirm.

Prompt

I wrapped my causal filter as a PyLops FunctionOperator to deconvolve with cgls, but cgls stalls: the cost barely decreases after the first few iterations and the model looks nothing like the truth. pylops.utils.dottest(Op, 300, 300) also fails. What's wrong with my code?

import numpy as np
import pylops

nt = 300
h = np.exp(-np.arange(30) / 6.0)  # causal, decaying filter

def forward(x):
    return np.convolve(x, h, mode="full")[:nt]

def adjoint(y):
    return np.convolve(y, h, mode="full")[:nt]

Op = pylops.FunctionOperator(forward, adjoint, nt, nt)

rng = np.random.default_rng(3)
xtrue = np.zeros(nt)
xtrue[[40, 90, 150, 220]] = [1.0, -0.7, 0.5, 0.9]

print(np.linalg.norm(xinv - xtrue) / np.linalg.norm(xtrue))
Graders — what "good" means for this case
answerllm

Ground truth: the forward operator is a causal convolution (np.convolve(x, h, "full")[:nt]), but the adjoint repeats the same convolution instead of correlating: the adjoint must use the time-reversed filter, e.g. np.convolve(y, h[::-1], "full")[len(h) - 1 : len(h) - 1 + nt] (or np.correlate(y, h, "full") with the matching slice), or the user can use pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D directly. With a correct adjoint cgls converges.

PASS only if ALL of these hold:

  • It says the adjoint is wrong (not the transpose of the forward) and that this is why the dot-test fails and cgls stalls.
  • It identifies the missing time reversal / correlation (and the corresponding slice) and gives a corrected adjoint equivalent to the ground truth, or recommends Convolve1D.
  • It does not name the solver choice, damp, niter or noise as the main cause.

Otherwise FAIL.

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01ViaihTJ5gKoykjAP798uto","type":"tool_result","content":"\"\"\"Template for choosing a regularisation weight in a PyLops inversion.\n\nRuns as-is on a toy 1-D deconvolution with second-derivative (smoothness)\nregularisation. To adapt it, replace ``build_problem`` and ``solve`` and keep\nthe sweep, the selection criteria and the plots.\n\nCriteria implemented:\n- L-curve corner (maximum curvature in log-log);\n- discrepancy principle (needs the noise standard deviation);\n- best SNR against a known model (synthetic tests only);\n- GCV, exact on dense matrices (small quadratic problems only).\n\"\"\"\n\nimport matplotlib.pyplot as plt\nimport numpy as np\n\nimport pylops\nfrom pylops.utils.metrics import snr\n\n\ndef build_problem(rng):\n    \"\"\"ADAPT: return (Op, Reg, y, xtrue or None, noise_std or None).\"\"\"\n    nt = 201\n    t = np.arange(nt) * 0.004\n    h, _, hcenter = pylops.utils.wavelets.ricker(t[:41], f0=15)\n    Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nt, h=h, offset=hcenter)\n    Reg = pylops.SecondDerivative(nt)\n\n    xtrue = np.sin(2 * np.pi * 3 * t) * np.exp(-t)  # smooth model\n    noise_std = 0.05\n    y = Op @ xtrue + noise_std * rng.standard_normal(nt)\n    return Op, Reg, y, xtrue, noise_std\n\n\ndef solve(Op, Reg, y, eps, x0):\n    \"\"\"ADAPT: the user's solver call with everything but ``eps`` fixed.\"\"\"\n    Opaug = pylops.VStack([Op, eps * Reg])\n    yaug = np.concatenate([y, np.zeros(Reg.shape[0])])\n    return pylops.cgls(Opaug, yaug, x0=x0, niter=200, tol=1e-10)[0]\n\n\ndef lcurve_corner(epsilons, rnorm, xnorm):\n    \"\"\"Index of the L-curve corner (maximum curvature in log-log).\n\n    Points are ordered by increasing eps so that the corner between the\n    \"under-regularised\" (vertical) and \"over-regularised\" (horizontal) branches\n    has positive curvature. A second knee with opposite curvature can appear\n    at very large eps (model pushed into the null space of the regulariser):\n    it is not the L-curve corner.\n    \"\"\"\n    order = np.argsort(epsilons)\n    lr, lx = np.log(rnorm[order]), np.log(xnorm[order])\n    # parametrise by arc length to make finite differences uniform\n    s = np.concatenate([[0], np.cumsum(np.hypot(np.diff(lr), np.diff(lx)))])\n    dr, dx = np.gradient(lr, s), np.gradient(lx, s)\n    ddr, ddx = np.gradient(dr, s), np.gradient(dx, s)\n    kappa = (dr * ddx - ddr * dx) / (dr**2 + dx**2) ** 1.5\n    kappa[[0, -1]] = -np.inf  # ends are not valid corners\n    kappa_orig = np.empty_like(kappa)\n    kappa_orig[order] = kappa\n    return int(np.argmax(kappa_orig)), kappa_orig\n\n\ndef discrepancy(epsilons, rnorm, noise_std, n, tau=1.02):\n    \"\"\"Largest eps whose residual is below tau * noise level (None if none).\"\"\"\n    target = tau * noise_std * np.sqrt(n)\n    ok = np.where(rnorm <= target)[0]\n    return (int(ok[np.argmax(epsilons[ok])]) if ok.size else None), target\n\n\ndef gcv_dense(Op, Reg, y, epsilons):\n    \"\"\"Exact GCV for min ||y - A x||^2 + eps^2 ||R x||^2 (small problems).\"\"\"\n    A, R = Op.todense(), Reg.todense()\n    AtA, RtR, n = A.T.conj() @ A, R.T.conj() @ R, A.shape[0]\n    g = []\n    for eps in epsilons:\n        H = A @ np.linalg.solve(AtA + eps**2 * RtR, A.T.conj())  # influence matrix\n        r = y - H @ y\n        g.append(n * np.real(r.conj() @ r) / np.real(n - np.trace(H)) ** 2)\n    return int(np.argmin(g)), np.array(g)\n\n\ndef main(neps=25, seed=0, dense_gcv=True):\n    rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)\n    Op, Reg, y, xtrue, noise_std = build_problem(rng)\n\n    # log grid around a scale-based guess, solved from large to small eps\n    eps0 = np.linalg.norm(Op.H @ y) / np.linalg.norm(Reg.H @ (Reg @ (Op.H @ y)))\n    epsilons = eps0 * np.logspace(3, -3, neps)\n\n    xs, rnorm, xnorm = [], [], []\n    x0 = np.zeros(Op.shape[1], dtype=Op.dtype)\n    for eps in epsilons:\n        x = solve(Op, Reg, y, eps, x0)\n        x0 = x  # warm start\n        xs.append(x)\n        rnorm.append(np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ x))\n        xnorm.append(np.linalg.norm(Reg @ x))\n    rnorm, xnorm = np.array(rnorm), np.array(xnorm)\n\n    picks = {}\n    icorner, _ = lcurve_corner(epsilons, rnorm, xnorm)\n    picks[\"L-curve\"] = icorner\n    if noise_std is not None:\n        idisc, target = discrepancy(epsilons, rnorm, noise_std, y.size)\n        if idisc is None:\n            print(f\"discrepancy: no eps reaches residual {target:.3e}; extend the grid\")\n        else:\n            picks[\"discrepancy\"] = idisc\n    snrs = None\n    if xtrue is not None:\n        snrs = np.array([snr(xtrue, x) for x in xs])\n        picks[\"best SNR\"] = int(np.argmax(snrs))\n    if dense_gcv and max(Op.shape) <= 3000:\n        igcv, gcv = gcv_dense(Op, Reg, y, epsilons)\n        picks[\"GCV\"] = igcv\n\n    for name, i in picks.items():\n        extra = f\", SNR={snrs[i]:.2f} dB\" if snrs is not None else \"\"\n        print(f\"{name:12s}: eps={epsilons[i]:.3e}, ||r||={rnorm[i]:.3e}{extra}\")\n\n    fig, axs = plt.subplots(1, 3, figsize=(15, 4))\n    axs[0].loglog(rnorm, xnorm, \".-k\")\n    for name, i in picks.items():\n        axs[0].log

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /Users/mrava87/Desktop/OpenSource/pylops-agent-skills/plugins/pylops/skills/tune-regularization\n\nGoal: a **sweep script** in the user's project that evaluates a regularisation\nparameter over a range, applies one or more selection criteria, and reports the\nchosen value with a plot that justifies it. PyLops has no built-in L-curve / GCV /\ndiscrepancy helper, so the skill provides `reference/lcurve_template.py`.\n\nRequires a working inversion (operator passes the dot-test, solver runs). If there\nis none, use `solve-inverse-problem` first.\n\n## 0. Check the environment\n\nSame as the other PyLops skills: use the project's Python runner, check\n`pylops.__version__`, inspect solver signatures in the installed version.\n\n## 1. Identify the parameter(s)\n\nMap the user's solver to the knob being tuned and its meaning:\n\n| Solver | Parameter | Objective term |\n|---|---|---|\n| `cgls` / `lsqr` | `damp` | `damp² ‖x‖²` |\n| `regularized_inversion` | `epsRs=[ε₁, ε₂, …]` | `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` |\n| `normal_equations_inversion` | `epsI`, `epsRs` (and `epsNRs`) | `epsI² ‖x‖²`, `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` (same scaling as `regularized_inversion`) |\n| `ista` / `fista` | `eps` | `eps ‖SOpᴴ x‖₁` (effective threshold is `eps·alpha/2`) |\n| `splitbregman` | `epsRL1s`, `epsRL2s`, `mu` | L1 and L2 weights, data weight |\n| `spgl1` | `sigma` (BPDN) or `tau` (LASSO) | residual bound or L1-ball radius |\n| any Krylov solver without explicit regularisation | `niter` | early stopping (semi-convergence) |\n\nWith more than one weight, tune one at a time (fix the others at a\nscale-based guess), or fix their ratio and sweep an overall factor. Do not\nlaunch a full grid over several parameters unless the problem is small.\n\n## 2. Pick the criterion\n\nChoose based on what the user knows:\n\n1. **Known noise level** (sigma per sample, or an estimate from a noise-only\n   window): **discrepancy principle** - choose the largest regularisation whose\n   data residual `‖y − Op x‖` is ≤ `τ · σ · sqrt(N)`, `τ ≈ 1.0–1.1`. For\n   `spgl1`, this is exactly `sigma = τ σ sqrt(N)`.\n2. **Synthetic with a true model**: maximise `pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, x)`\n   (report it, but warn it does not transfer 1:1 to field data).\n3. **Nothing known**: **L-curve** - plot `log ‖Rx‖` vs `log ‖y − Op x‖` and pick the\n   point of maximum curvature. Needs a wide enough range to show both branches.\n4. **Small problem** (`Op.todense()` affordable, N, M ≲ 3000) and quadratic\n   regularisation: **GCV** computed exactly on the dense matrices (implemented in\n   the template).\n\nReport more than one criterion when they are cheap; disagreement between them is\ninformation for the user.\n\n## 3. Write and run the sweep\n\nAdapt `reference/lcurve_template.py`:\n\n- replace `build_problem` with the user's operator, data and regularisation;\n- replace `solve(eps, x0)` with the user's solver call **with everything else\n  fixed** (same `niter`, tolerance, `x0` policy);\n- use a **log-spaced grid** spanning at least 4 orders of magnitude around a\n  scale-based guess (e.g. `ε₀ ≈ ‖Opᴴ y‖ / ‖Rᴴ R Opᴴ y‖` for quadratic terms, or\n  `eps₀ ≈ max|Opᴴ y|` scale for L1 thresholds, where larger values give all-zero\n  models);\n- solve from large to small ε and **warm start** from the previous solution\n  (`x0=...`) to save iterations - unless the solver ignores `x0`;\n- keep `niter` large enough that each solve has converged, otherwise the curve\n  measures iteration count, not regularisation (check a couple of cost curves);\n- for early stopping as regulariser, run once with a callback that stores\n  `x` every k iterations and compute the same criteria on iteration number.\n\nRun it, then look at the plot before trusting the automatic pick: an L-curve with\nno clear corner (common with L1 terms or very noisy data) should be reported as\nsuch, not forced. With derivative regularisers a second knee often appears at\nvery large ε (the model collapses into the null space of `R`, e.g. a linear\ntrend for `SecondDerivative`); it bends the other way and is not the L-curve\ncorner – the template's curvature orientation already excludes it.\n\n## 4. Hand off\n\nReport:\n\n- the parameter swept, the range and the number of solves;\n- the value picked by each criterion and the one recommended, with the reason;\n- the plot file(s) (L-curve, residual vs ε, SNR vs ε if available) and the\n  model at the chosen value next to one over- and one under-regularised model;\n- the exact line to change in the user's inversion script.\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"754bde06-0775-4e16-98e9-c65a8925ded9","uuid":"1733ede2-cb5b-4433-81cf-e7e2698cd716","timestamp":"2026-10-31T15:16:41.736Z","isSynthetic":true}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01YHFTAxJBJ5VsbZFwVwJbv7","type":"tool_result","content":"\"\"\"Template for choosing a regularisation weight in a PyLops inversion.\n\nRuns as-is on a toy 1-D deconvolution with second-derivative (smoothness)\nregularisation. To adapt it, replace ``build_problem`` and ``solve`` and keep\nthe sweep, the selection criteria and the plots.\n\nCriteria implemented:\n- L-curve corner (maximum curvature in log-log);\n- discrepancy principle (needs the noise standard deviation);\n- best SNR against a known model (synthetic tests only);\n- GCV, exact on dense matrices (small quadratic problems only).\n\"\"\"\n\nimport matplotlib.pyplot as plt\nimport numpy as np\n\nimport pylops\nfrom pylops.utils.metrics import snr\n\n\ndef build_problem(rng):\n    \"\"\"ADAPT: return (Op, Reg, y, xtrue or None, noise_std or None).\"\"\"\n    nt = 201\n    t = np.arange(nt) * 0.004\n    h, _, hcenter = pylops.utils.wavelets.ricker(t[:41], f0=15)\n    Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nt, h=h, offset=hcenter)\n    Reg = pylops.SecondDerivative(nt)\n\n    xtrue = np.sin(2 * np.pi * 3 * t) * np.exp(-t)  # smooth model\n    noise_std = 0.05\n    y = Op @ xtrue + noise_std * rng.standard_normal(nt)\n    return Op, Reg, y, xtrue, noise_std\n\n\ndef solve(Op, Reg, y, eps, x0):\n    \"\"\"ADAPT: the user's solver call with everything but ``eps`` fixed.\"\"\"\n    Opaug = pylops.VStack([Op, eps * Reg])\n    yaug = np.concatenate([y, np.zeros(Reg.shape[0])])\n    return pylops.cgls(Opaug, yaug, x0=x0, niter=200, tol=1e-10)[0]\n\n\ndef lcurve_corner(epsilons, rnorm, xnorm):\n    \"\"\"Index of the L-curve corner (maximum curvature in log-log).\n\n    Points are ordered by increasing eps so that the corner between the\n    \"under-regularised\" (vertical) and \"over-regularised\" (horizontal) branches\n    has positive curvature. A second knee with opposite curvature can appear\n    at very large eps (model pushed into the null space of the regulariser):\n    it is not the L-curve corner.\n    \"\"\"\n    order = np.argsort(epsilons)\n    lr, lx = np.log(rnorm[order]), np.log(xnorm[order])\n    # parametrise by arc length to make finite differences uniform\n    s = np.concatenate([[0], np.cumsum(np.hypot(np.diff(lr), np.diff(lx)))])\n    dr, dx = np.gradient(lr, s), np.gradient(lx, s)\n    ddr, ddx = np.gradient(dr, s), np.gradient(dx, s)\n    kappa = (dr * ddx - ddr * dx) / (dr**2 + dx**2) ** 1.5\n    kappa[[0, -1]] = -np.inf  # ends are not valid corners\n    kappa_orig = np.empty_like(kappa)\n    kappa_orig[order] = kappa\n    return int(np.argmax(kappa_orig)), kappa_orig\n\n\ndef discrepancy(epsilons, rnorm, noise_std, n, tau=1.02):\n    \"\"\"Largest eps whose residual is below tau * noise level (None if none).\"\"\"\n    target = tau * noise_std * np.sqrt(n)\n    ok = np.where(rnorm <= target)[0]\n    return (int(ok[np.argmax(epsilons[ok])]) if ok.size else None), target\n\n\ndef gcv_dense(Op, Reg, y, epsilons):\n    \"\"\"Exact GCV for min ||y - A x||^2 + eps^2 ||R x||^2 (small problems).\"\"\"\n    A, R = Op.todense(), Reg.todense()\n    AtA, RtR, n = A.T.conj() @ A, R.T.conj() @ R, A.shape[0]\n    g = []\n    for eps in epsilons:\n        H = A @ np.linalg.solve(AtA + eps**2 * RtR, A.T.conj())  # influence matrix\n        r = y - H @ y\n        g.append(n * np.real(r.conj() @ r) / np.real(n - np.trace(H)) ** 2)\n    return int(np.argmin(g)), np.array(g)\n\n\ndef main(neps=25, seed=0, dense_gcv=True):\n    rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)\n    Op, Reg, y, xtrue, noise_std = build_problem(rng)\n\n    # log grid around a scale-based guess, solved from large to small eps\n    eps0 = np.linalg.norm(Op.H @ y) / np.linalg.norm(Reg.H @ (Reg @ (Op.H @ y)))\n    epsilons = eps0 * np.logspace(3, -3, neps)\n\n    xs, rnorm, xnorm = [], [], []\n    x0 = np.zeros(Op.shape[1], dtype=Op.dtype)\n    for eps in epsilons:\n        x = solve(Op, Reg, y, eps, x0)\n        x0 = x  # warm start\n        xs.append(x)\n        rnorm.append(np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ x))\n        xnorm.append(np.linalg.norm(Reg @ x))\n    rnorm, xnorm = np.array(rnorm), np.array(xnorm)\n\n    picks = {}\n    icorner, _ = lcurve_corner(epsilons, rnorm, xnorm)\n    picks[\"L-curve\"] = icorner\n    if noise_std is not None:\n        idisc, target = discrepancy(epsilons, rnorm, noise_std, y.size)\n        if idisc is None:\n            print(f\"discrepancy: no eps reaches residual {target:.3e}; extend the grid\")\n        else:\n            picks[\"discrepancy\"] = idisc\n    snrs = None\n    if xtrue is not None:\n        snrs = np.array([snr(xtrue, x) for x in xs])\n        picks[\"best SNR\"] = int(np.argmax(snrs))\n    if dense_gcv and max(Op.shape) <= 3000:\n        igcv, gcv = gcv_dense(Op, Reg, y, epsilons)\n        picks[\"GCV\"] = igcv\n\n    for name, i in picks.items():\n        extra = f\", SNR={snrs[i]:.2f} dB\" if snrs is not None else \"\"\n        print(f\"{name:12s}: eps={epsilons[i]:.3e}, ||r||={rnorm[i]:.3e}{extra}\")\n\n    fig, axs = plt.subplots(1, 3, figsize=(15, 4))\n    axs[0].loglog(rnorm, xnorm, \".-k\")\n    for name, i in picks.items():\n        axs[0

PyLops has no built-in L-curve / GCV /\ndiscrepancy helper, so the skill provides `reference/lcurve_template.py`.\n\nRequires a working inversion (operator passes the dot-test, solver runs). If there\nis none, use `solve-inverse-problem` first.\n\n## 0. Check the environment\n\nSame as the other PyLops skills: use the project's Python runner, check\n`pylops.__version__`, inspect solver signatures in the installed version.\n\n## 1. Identify the parameter(s)\n\nMap the user's solver to the knob being tuned and its meaning:\n\n| Solver | Parameter | Objective term |\n|---|---|---|\n| `cgls` / `lsqr` | `damp` | `damp² ‖x‖²` |\n| `regularized_inversion` | `epsRs=[ε₁, ε₂, …]` | `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` |\n| `normal_equations_inversion` | `epsI`, `epsRs` (and `epsNRs`) | `epsI² ‖x‖²`, `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` (same scaling as `regularized_inversion`) |\n| `ista` / `fista` | `eps` | `eps ‖SOpᴴ x‖₁` (effective threshold is `eps·alpha/2`) |\n| `splitbregman` | `epsRL1s`, `epsRL2s`, `mu` | L1 and L2 weights, data weight |\n| `spgl1` | `sigma` (BPDN) or `tau` (LASSO) | residual bound or L1-ball radius |\n| any Krylov solver without explicit regularisation | `niter` | early stopping (semi-convergence) |\n\nWith more than one weight, tune one at a time (fix the others at a\nscale-based guess), or fix their ratio and sweep an overall factor. Do not\nlaunch a full grid over several parameters unless the problem is small.\n\n## 2. Pick the criterion\n\nChoose based on what the user knows:\n\n1. **Known noise level** (sigma per sample, or an estimate from a noise-only\n   window): **discrepancy principle** - choose the largest regularisation whose\n   data residual `‖y − Op x‖` is ≤ `τ · σ · sqrt(N)`, `τ ≈ 1.0–1.1`. For\n   `spgl1`, this is exactly `sigma = τ σ sqrt(N)`.\n2. **Synthetic with a true model**: maximise `pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, x)`\n   (report it, but warn it does not transfer 1:1 to field data).\n3. **Nothing known**: **L-curve** - plot `log ‖Rx‖` vs `log ‖y − Op x‖` and pick the\n   point of maximum curvature. Needs a wide enough range to show both branches.\n4. **Small problem** (`Op.todense()` affordable, N, M ≲ 3000) and quadratic\n   regularisation: **GCV** computed exactly on the dense matrices (implemented in\n   the template).\n\nReport more than one criterion when they are cheap; disagreement between them is\ninformation for the user.\n\n## 3. Write and run the sweep\n\nAdapt `reference/lcurve_template.py`:\n\n- replace `build_problem` with the user's operator, data and regularisation;\n- replace `solve(eps, x0)` with the user's solver call **with everything else\n  fixed** (same `niter`, tolerance, `x0` policy);\n- use a **log-spaced grid** spanning at least 4 orders of magnitude around a\n  scale-based guess (e.g. `ε₀ ≈ ‖Opᴴ y‖ / ‖Rᴴ R Opᴴ y‖` for quadratic terms, or\n  `eps₀ ≈ max|Opᴴ y|` scale for L1 thresholds, where larger values give all-zero\n  models);\n- solve from large to small ε and **warm start** from the previous solution\n  (`x0=...`) to save iterations - unless the solver ignores `x0`;\n- keep `niter` large enough that each solve has converged, otherwise the curve\n  measures iteration count, not regularisation (check a couple of cost curves);\n- for early stopping as regulariser, run once with a callback that stores\n  `x` every k iterations and compute the same criteria on iteration number.\n\nRun it, then look at the plot before trusting the automatic pick: an L-curve with\nno clear corner (common with L1 terms or very noisy data) should be reported as\nsuch, not forced. With derivative regularisers a second knee often appears at\nvery large ε (the model collapses into the null space of `R`, e.g. a linear\ntrend for `SecondDerivative`); it bends the other way and is not the L-curve\ncorner – the template's curvature orientation already excludes it.\n\n## 4. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01Gft1jmLdJykdwSxdwNcfKv","type":"tool_result","content":"\"\"\"Template for choosing a regularisation weight in a PyLops inversion.\n\nRuns as-is on a toy 1-D deconvolution with second-derivative (smoothness)\nregularisation. To adapt it, replace ``build_problem`` and ``solve`` and keep\nthe sweep, the selection criteria and the plots.\n\nCriteria implemented:\n- L-curve corner (maximum curvature in log-log);\n- discrepancy principle (needs the noise standard deviation);\n- best SNR against a known model (synthetic tests only);\n- GCV, exact on dense matrices (small quadratic problems only).\n\"\"\"\n\nimport matplotlib.pyplot as plt\nimport numpy as np\n\nimport pylops\nfrom pylops.utils.metrics import snr\n\n\ndef build_problem(rng):\n    \"\"\"ADAPT: return (Op, Reg, y, xtrue or None, noise_std or None).\"\"\"\n    nt = 201\n    t = np.arange(nt) * 0.004\n    h, _, hcenter = pylops.utils.wavelets.ricker(t[:41], f0=15)\n    Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nt, h=h, offset=hcenter)\n    Reg = pylops.SecondDerivative(nt)\n\n    xtrue = np.sin(2 * np.pi * 3 * t) * np.exp(-t)  # smooth model\n    noise_std = 0.05\n    y = Op @ xtrue + noise_std * rng.standard_normal(nt)\n    return Op, Reg, y, xtrue, noise_std\n\n\ndef solve(Op, Reg, y, eps, x0):\n    \"\"\"ADAPT: the user's solver call with everything but ``eps`` fixed.\"\"\"\n    Opaug = pylops.VStack([Op, eps * Reg])\n    yaug = np.concatenate([y, np.zeros(Reg.shape[0])])\n    return pylops.cgls(Opaug, yaug, x0=x0, niter=200, tol=1e-10)[0]\n\n\ndef lcurve_corner(epsilons, rnorm, xnorm):\n    \"\"\"Index of the L-curve corner (maximum curvature in log-log).\n\n    Points are ordered by increasing eps so that the corner between the\n    \"under-regularised\" (vertical) and \"over-regularised\" (horizontal) branches\n    has positive curvature. A second knee with opposite curvature can appear\n    at very large eps (model pushed into the null space of the regulariser):\n    it is not the L-curve corner.\n    \"\"\"\n    order = np.argsort(epsilons)\n    lr, lx = np.log(rnorm[order]), np.log(xnorm[order])\n    # parametrise by arc length to make finite differences uniform\n    s = np.concatenate([[0], np.cumsum(np.hypot(np.diff(lr), np.diff(lx)))])\n    dr, dx = np.gradient(lr, s), np.gradient(lx, s)\n    ddr, ddx = np.gradient(dr, s), np.gradient(dx, s)\n    kappa = (dr * ddx - ddr * dx) / (dr**2 + dx**2) ** 1.5\n    kappa[[0, -1]] = -np.inf  # ends are not valid corners\n    kappa_orig = np.empty_like(kappa)\n    kappa_orig[order] = kappa\n    return int(np.argmax(kappa_orig)), kappa_orig\n\n\ndef discrepancy(epsilons, rnorm, noise_std, n, tau=1.02):\n    \"\"\"Largest eps whose residual is below tau * noise level (None if none).\"\"\"\n    target = tau * noise_std * np.sqrt(n)\n    ok = np.where(rnorm <= target)[0]\n    return (int(ok[np.argmax(epsilons[ok])]) if ok.size else None), target\n\n\ndef gcv_dense(Op, Reg, y, epsilons):\n    \"\"\"Exact GCV for min ||y - A x||^2 + eps^2 ||R x||^2 (small problems).\"\"\"\n    A, R = Op.todense(), Reg.todense()\n    AtA, RtR, n = A.T.conj() @ A, R.T.conj() @ R, A.shape[0]\n    g = []\n    for eps in epsilons:\n        H = A @ np.linalg.solve(AtA + eps**2 * RtR, A.T.conj())  # influence matrix\n        r = y - H @ y\n        g.append(n * np.real(r.conj() @ r) / np.real(n - np.trace(H)) ** 2)\n    return int(np.argmin(g)), np.array(g)\n\n\ndef main(neps=25, seed=0, dense_gcv=True):\n    rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)\n    Op, Reg, y, xtrue, noise_std = build_problem(rng)\n\n    # log grid around a scale-based guess, solved from large to small eps\n    eps0 = np.linalg.norm(Op.H @ y) / np.linalg.norm(Reg.H @ (Reg @ (Op.H @ y)))\n    epsilons = eps0 * np.logspace(3, -3, neps)\n\n    xs, rnorm, xnorm = [], [], []\n    x0 = np.zeros(Op.shape[1], dtype=Op.dtype)\n    for eps in epsilons:\n        x = solve(Op, Reg, y, eps, x0)\n        x0 = x  # warm start\n        xs.append(x)\n        rnorm.append(np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ x))\n        xnorm.append(np.linalg.norm(Reg @ x))\n    rnorm, xnorm = np.array(rnorm), np.array(xnorm)\n\n    picks = {}\n    icorner, _ = lcurve_corner(epsilons, rnorm, xnorm)\n    picks[\"L-curve\"] = icorner\n    if noise_std is not None:\n        idisc, target = discrepancy(epsilons, rnorm, noise_std, y.size)\n        if idisc is None:\n            print(f\"discrepancy: no eps reaches residual {target:.3e}; extend the grid\")\n        else:\n            picks[\"discrepancy\"] = idisc\n    snrs = None\n    if xtrue is not None:\n        snrs = np.array([snr(xtrue, x) for x in xs])\n        picks[\"best SNR\"] = int(np.argmax(snrs))\n    if dense_gcv and max(Op.shape) <= 3000:\n        igcv, gcv = gcv_dense(Op, Reg, y, epsilons)\n        picks[\"GCV\"] = igcv\n\n    for name, i in picks.items():\n        extra = f\", SNR={snrs[i]:.2f} dB\" if snrs is not None else \"\"\n        print(f\"{name:12s}: eps={epsilons[i]:.3e}, ||r||={rnorm[i]:.3e}{extra}\")\n\n    fig, axs = plt.subplots(1, 3, figsize=(15, 4))\n    axs[0].loglog(rnorm, xnorm, \".-k\")\n    for name, i in picks.items():\n        a

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /private/tmp/e-dz3axU/tmp/claude-501/bundled-skills/2.1.316/18201e6e73817a0d72e89fb894c7871d/dataviz\n\n# Data Visualization\n\nA chart is **read by people and executed by you**. This skill turns \"make it look\ngood\" into a procedure with checks, so the result is right by construction rather\nthan by taste.\n\n**The method here is design-system-agnostic.** Nothing in the procedure, the form\nheuristic, the six checks, or the mark specs is specific to one product. A design\nsystem supplies a small set of *parameters* (its ramps, a categorical order, a\ndiverging pair, a status palette, a texture, its surfaces, its filter components);\nthe method consumes them unchanged. A **validated default palette** is the\nreference instance, fully specified in `references/palette.md`. To target your\nbrand, read that file's structure and substitute its values - touch nothing else.\n\n> The single most important habit: **the color part is computable, so compute it.**\n> Never eyeball whether a palette is colorblind-safe - run `scripts/validate_palette.js`.\n\n## The procedure - do these in order\n\nColor comes LAST. Most bad charts pick colors first.\n\n1. **Pick the form.** What is the data's job - magnitude, identity, polarity, a\n   single headline, change-over-time? The job picks the chart type, and sometimes\n   the answer is *not a chart* (a stat tile or hero number). -> `references/choosing-a-form.md`\n2. **Assign color by the job it does.** Categorical (identity), sequential\n   (magnitude), diverging (polarity), or status (state) - each has one rule.\n   Assign categorical hues in fixed order, never cycled. -> `references/color-formula.md`\n3. **VALIDATE the palette - run the script, don't reason about Delta E.**\n   `node scripts/validate_palette.js \"<hex,hex,...>\" --mode light` (relative to\n   this skill's base directory - or load it as `<script type=\"module\">` in the\n   chart's own page, where it reads\n   `data-palette` off `<body>` and logs a `console.table` report). It returns\n   pass/fail on the lightness band, chroma floor, adjacent-pair CVD separation,\n   the normal-vision floor, and contrast. Fix anything that FAILs before continuing. Re-run for\n   `--mode dark` with that mode's surface.\n4. **Apply mark specs & spacers.** Thin marks, 4px rounded data-ends anchored to\n   the baseline, 2px lines, >=8px markers, a 2px surface gap between fills (stacked\n   segments and adjacent bars alike) and a 2px surface ring on overlapping marks,\n   selective direct labels. -> `references/marks-and-anatomy.md`\n5. **Add the hover layer - by default.** An HTML/SVG chart *is* interactive; ship\n   a crosshair+tooltip on line/area and a per-mark hover tooltip on bar/dot/cell.\n   The only form that skips it is a bare stat tile with no plot. Hit targets bigger\n   than the mark; filters in one row above the charts. -> `references/interaction.md`\n6. **Final accessibility pass.** For >= 2 series a legend is always present and <= 4\n   are also direct-labeled (a single series needs no legend box - the title names\n   it), so identity is never color-alone; a table view exists; dark mode is **selected** - its own\n   steps from the same ramps, validated against the dark surface, not an automatic\n   flip; texture is available for the CVD/print/forced-colors case.\n7. **Render it and look at it.** The validator checks color, not layout - open or\n   screenshot the output and eyeball it for label collisions, geometry, and overflow\n   before calling it done.\n\nThen check the result against **`references/anti-patterns.md`** - it is the catalog\nof what goes wrong. If your chart matches an entry, it's wrong.\n\n## Non-negotiables (true in every design system)\n\n- **Assign categorical hues in fixed order, never cycled.** A 9th series is never a\n  generated hue - it folds into \"Other,\" small multiples, or composite encoding.\n- **One axis.** Never a dual-axis chart (two y-scales). Two measures of different\n  scale -> two charts, small multiples, or indexed to a common base. *(This is the\n  #1 chart mistake - see anti-patterns.)*\n- **Color follows the entity, never its rank.** A filter that changes the series\n  count must not repaint the survivors.\n- **Sequential = one hue, light->dark. Diverging = two hues + a neutral gray\n  midpoint.** Never a rainbow; never a hue at the diverging midpoint.\n- **Run the validator before shipping any categorical palette.** CVD Delta E >= 8 is the\n  target (OKLab ×100); 6-8 is a floor that is legal ONLY with secondary encoding. A\n  normal-vision floor below 15 is a hard FAIL - full-color readers can't tell the\n  pair apart; re-step it on the adjacent pairlist (secondary encoding does not excuse\n  this one); under `--pairs all` cut series or facet instead - see check 4. A contrast WARN\n  obligates visible labels or a table view - it is not dismissable.\n- **Thin marks; a legend always present for >= 2 series (none for one), with\n  selective direct labels (never a number on every point); recessive grid/axes.**\n  One exception, legend kept: the tall segmented bar (*Figures* in `references/marks-and-anatomy.md`).\n- **Text wears text tokens, never the series color** - values, labels, and legends\n  stay in primary/secondary/muted ink; a colored mark beside them carries identity.\n- **Status colors are reserved** (good/warning/serious/critical) and never reused\n  for \"series 4\"; they ship with an icon + label, never color alone.\n\n## Plugging in a design system\n\nThe method is invariant; only these parameters change per system. The reference\ninstance - every value filled in - is `references/palette.md`.\n\n| Parameter | What the system provides |\n|---|---|\n| **Ramps** | the hue scales (named steps) the palette draws from |\n| **Categorical theme** | the fixed hue order (a named theme); default + alternates |\n| **Sequential hue** | the default single hue for magnitude |\n| **Diverging pair** | two warm/cool poles + a neutral midpoint |\n| **Status palette** | good / warning / serious / critical - steps distinct from categorical |\n| **Texture fill** | one directional hand-drawn fill, used at 45° / 135° |\n| **Surfaces** | light & dark chart-surface colors (the validator needs these) |\n| **Filter controls** | date-range & dimension controls (behavioral spec in `interaction.md`) |\n\nTo onboard a new system: fill those rows, feed its ramps to the validator, and let\nit snap each slot to the nearest passing step. Structure and rules stay as written.\n\n## Reference files\n\n| File | What it answers |\n|------|-----------------|\n| `references/choosing-a-form.md` | Which chart type / is it even a chart? |\n| `references/color-formula.md` | The four jobs, the six checks, snap-to-passing |\n| `references/marks-and-anatomy.md` | Mark specs, spacers, labels, figures, hero number |\n| `references/interaction.md` | Tooltips & hover, filters & time ranges |\n| `references/components.md` | The pieces a chart is made of - build each in plain HTML |\n| `references/anti-patterns.md` | **What goes wrong - check every chart against this** |\n| `references/palette.md` | **The reference palette instance** - every parameter, filled in; swap for your brand's |\n| `scripts/validate_palette.js` | Runnable six-checks validator (run it; don't eyeball) |\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"c14a8f51-7e7e-41a8-a418-05467f18d6d6","uuid":"62708f35-fe55-47dd-9cbd-814f1e8f7deb","timestamp":"2026-10-31T15:16:56.845Z","isSynthetic":true}

reg.append(np.linalg.norm(D2@x))\n    err.append(np.linalg.norm(x-xtrue)/np.linalg.norm(xtrue))\nres, reg, err = map(np.array, (res, reg, err))\n\n# corner: max curvature in log-log space\nlr, lg = np.log(res), np.log(reg)\ns = np.log(eps)\nd1r, d1g = np.gradient(lr, s), np.gradient(lg, s)\nd2r, d2g = np.gradient(d1r, s), np.gradient(d1g, s)\ncurv = (d1r*d2g - d1g*d2r)/(d1r**2+d1g**2)**1.5\nic = np.argmax(np.abs(curv[2:-2]))+2\n# discrepancy: ||Op x - y|| ~ sigma*sqrt(n)\ntarget = sigma*np.sqrt(n)\nidp = np.argmin(np.abs(res-target))\nprint(\"L-curve corner eps=%.3g  err=%.4f\" % (eps[ic], err[ic]))\nprint(\"discrepancy eps=%.3g  err=%.4f\" % (eps[idp], err[idp]))\nprint(\"eps=1.0 err=%.4f\" % err[np.argmin(np.abs(eps-1))])\nprint(\"best-by-truth eps=%.3g err=%.4f\" % (eps[np.argmin(err)], err.min()))\nfig, ax = plt.subplots(1, 2, figsize=(10,4))\nax[0].loglog(res, reg, \"k.-\"); ax[0].loglog(res[ic], reg[ic], \"ro\", label=f\"corner eps={eps[ic]:.2g}\")\nax[0].loglog(res[idp], reg[idp], \"bs\", label=f\"discrepancy eps={eps[idp]:.2g}\")\nax[0].axvline(target, color=\"b\", ls=\":\", lw=.8)\nax[0].set_xlabel(\"||Op x - y||\"); ax[0].set_ylabel(\"||D2 x||\"); ax[0].legend()\nax[1].loglog(eps, err, \"k.-\"); ax[1].set_xlabel(\"epsR\"); ax[1].set_ylabel(\"rel. error vs truth\")\nplt.tight_layout(); plt.savefig(\"lcurve.png\", dpi=120)\nEOF\npython lcurve.py; {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /private/tmp/e-tgWwkA/tmp/claude-501/bundled-skills/2.1.316/75bf6611ce5284268647e0f7900099a7/dataviz\n\n# Data Visualization\n\nA chart is **read by people and executed by you**. This skill turns \"make it look\ngood\" into a procedure with checks, so the result is right by construction rather\nthan by taste.\n\n**The method here is design-system-agnostic.** Nothing in the procedure, the form\nheuristic, the six checks, or the mark specs is specific to one product. A design\nsystem supplies a small set of *parameters* (its ramps, a categorical order, a\ndiverging pair, a status palette, a texture, its surfaces, its filter components);\nthe method consumes them unchanged. A **validated default palette** is the\nreference instance, fully specified in `references/palette.md`. To target your\nbrand, read that file's structure and substitute its values - touch nothing else.\n\n> The single most important habit: **the color part is computable, so compute it.**\n> Never eyeball whether a palette is colorblind-safe - run `scripts/validate_palette.js`.\n\n## The procedure - do these in order\n\nColor comes LAST. Most bad charts pick colors first.\n\n1. **Pick the form.** What is the data's job - magnitude, identity, polarity, a\n   single headline, change-over-time? The job picks the chart type, and sometimes\n   the answer is *not a chart* (a stat tile or hero number). -> `references/choosing-a-form.md`\n2. **Assign color by the job it does.** Categorical (identity), sequential\n   (magnitude), diverging (polarity), or status (state) - each has one rule.\n   Assign categorical hues in fixed order, never cycled. -> `references/color-formula.md`\n3. **VALIDATE the palette - run the script, don't reason about Delta E.**\n   `node scripts/validate_palette.js \"<hex,hex,...>\" --mode light` (relative to\n   this skill's base directory - or load it as `<script type=\"module\">` in the\n   chart's own page, where it reads\n   `data-palette` off `<body>` and logs a `console.table` report). It returns\n   pass/fail on the lightness band, chroma floor, adjacent-pair CVD separation,\n   the normal-vision floor, and contrast. Fix anything that FAILs before continuing. Re-run for\n   `--mode dark` with that mode's surface.\n4. **Apply mark specs & spacers.** Thin marks, 4px rounded data-ends anchored to\n   the baseline, 2px lines, >=8px markers, a 2px surface gap between fills (stacked\n   segments and adjacent bars alike) and a 2px surface ring on overlapping marks,\n   selective direct labels. -> `references/marks-and-anatomy.md`\n5. **Add the hover layer - by default.** An HTML/SVG chart *is* interactive; ship\n   a crosshair+tooltip on line/area and a per-mark hover tooltip on bar/dot/cell.\n   The only form that skips it is a bare stat tile with no plot. Hit targets bigger\n   than the mark; filters in one row above the charts. -> `references/interaction.md`\n6. **Final accessibility pass.** For >= 2 series a legend is always present and <= 4\n   are also direct-labeled (a single series needs no legend box - the title names\n   it), so identity is never color-alone; a table view exists; dark mode is **selected** - its own\n   steps from the same ramps, validated against the dark surface, not an automatic\n   flip; texture is available for the CVD/print/forced-colors case.\n7. **Render it and look at it.** The validator checks color, not layout - open or\n   screenshot the output and eyeball it for label collisions, geometry, and overflow\n   before calling it done.\n\nThen check the result against **`references/anti-patterns.md`** - it is the catalog\nof what goes wrong. If your chart matches an entry, it's wrong.\n\n## Non-negotiables (true in every design system)\n\n- **Assign categorical hues in fixed order, never cycled.** A 9th series is never a\n  generated hue - it folds into \"Other,\" small multiples, or composite encoding.\n- **One axis.** Never a dual-axis chart (two y-scales). Two measures of different\n  scale -> two charts, small multiples, or indexed to a common base. *(This is the\n  #1 chart mistake - see anti-patterns.)*\n- **Color follows the entity, never its rank.** A filter that changes the series\n  count must not repaint the survivors.\n- **Sequential = one hue, light->dark. Diverging = two hues + a neutral gray\n  midpoint.** Never a rainbow; never a hue at the diverging midpoint.\n- **Run the validator before shipping any categorical palette.** CVD Delta E >= 8 is the\n  target (OKLab ×100); 6-8 is a floor that is legal ONLY with secondary encoding. A\n  normal-vision floor below 15 is a hard FAIL - full-color readers can't tell the\n  pair apart; re-step it on the adjacent pairlist (secondary encoding does not excuse\n  this one); under `--pairs all` cut series or facet instead - see check 4. A contrast WARN\n  obligates visible labels or a table view - it is not dismissable.\n- **Thin marks; a legend always present for >= 2 series (none for one), with\n  selective direct labels (never a number on every point); recessive grid/axes.**\n  One exception, legend kept: the tall segmented bar (*Figures* in `references/marks-and-anatomy.md`).\n- **Text wears text tokens, never the series color** - values, labels, and legends\n  stay in primary/secondary/muted ink; a colored mark beside them carries identity.\n- **Status colors are reserved** (good/warning/serious/critical) and never reused\n  for \"series 4\"; they ship with an icon + label, never color alone.\n\n## Plugging in a design system\n\nThe method is invariant; only these parameters change per system. The reference\ninstance - every value filled in - is `references/palette.md`.\n\n| Parameter | What the system provides |\n|---|---|\n| **Ramps** | the hue scales (named steps) the palette draws from |\n| **Categorical theme** | the fixed hue order (a named theme); default + alternates |\n| **Sequential hue** | the default single hue for magnitude |\n| **Diverging pair** | two warm/cool poles + a neutral midpoint |\n| **Status palette** | good / warning / serious / critical - steps distinct from categorical |\n| **Texture fill** | one directional hand-drawn fill, used at 45° / 135° |\n| **Surfaces** | light & dark chart-surface colors (the validator needs these) |\n| **Filter controls** | date-range & dimension controls (behavioral spec in `interaction.md`) |\n\nTo onboard a new system: fill those rows, feed its ramps to the validator, and let\nit snap each slot to the nearest passing step. Prompt

My smooth-model inversion with PyLops works fine, but I picked epsRs=[1.0] by eye and the result looks a bit too smooth. How should I choose epsRs properly? Please make me an L-curve for it and tell me which value to use. Here's the script:

import numpy as np
import pylops

rng = np.random.default_rng(5)
n = 200
z = np.arange(n)
xtrue = np.sin(2 * np.pi * z / 80) + 0.5 * np.exp(-0.5 * ((z - 120) / 6) ** 2)
t = np.arange(-15, 16)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 4) ** 2); h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=15)
y = Op @ xtrue + 0.02 * rng.standard_normal(n)

D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(n)
xinv = pylops.regularized_inversion(Op, y, [D2], epsRs=[1.0], iter_lim=200)[0]
print(np.linalg.norm(xinv - xtrue) / np.linalg.norm(xtrue))

  • It reports the recommended epsRs and the numbers backing it, and they do not visibly contradict the tool output in the trace (parts of the trace may be elided; do not fail for numbers you cannot find).
  • It keeps the user's solver and regulariser rather than re-designing the inversion (a brief side remark about alternatives is fine).

Otherwise FAIL.
